See the [host matrix](../testing/host-matrix.md).\\n\\nReading these files directly does not prove supported plugin loading, registration, invocation or host behavior. Both hosts remain not-run. Installation, dependency setup, host enabling, personal settings, schedules, credentials, listeners, commits and external writes retain their applicable explicit permission gates. A local draft is not a PR or verified app. Read the linked adapted workflow in full before its step; a missing required companion remains a blocked gate.\\n\\n\\nEditing code you don't understand is how subtle regressions ship, and that's as true for the agent as for you. Agents usually fail in one of two ways. They misread what you want, or they don't have the context to do the work right. [What goes in a prompt](./02-poteto-mode.md#what-goes-in-a-prompt) handles the first. This page handles the second.\\n\\npstack gives you four ways in. `/how` explains what the code does now. `/why` digs up the reasons it's shaped that way. `/teach` blends both into one explanation. `/recall` rebuilds your own recent context on a topic. Each one also makes the agent explain itself in words you can check. That's how you supervise an agent that may know the code better than you do.\\n\\n![A detective studies a machine blueprint with a magnifying glass while robots fetch case files; the evidence board behind her links clues under /how and /why.](./images/understanding.jpg)\\n\\n## Start with a read-only investigation\\n\\nWhen the cause is unclear, ask for findings, not a fix:\\n\\n```text\\n/poteto-mode investigate why background jobs time out every few hours. give me what we know, what data you used, and your best hypotheses. don't change any code yet.\\n```\\n\\n\\\"don't change any code yet\\\" routes this to the [Investigation playbook](../../skills/poteto-mode/playbooks/investigation.md). It runs `/how`, adds `/why` for questions about motivation, and returns a cited explanation. For a choice between options, it returns a recommendation with a trade-offs table. Asking \\\"what data you used\\\" makes the agent separate its evidence from its guesses. When the findings point at a fix, start the fix as a new task.\\n\\n## Trace behavior with `/how`\\n\\n```text\\n/how do we dedupe notifications? is there an n+1 when we look up subscribers?\\n```\\n\\nAsk the question you actually have. [`/how`](../../skills/how/SKILL.md) reads the code and answers at the level of a senior engineer onboarding you onto the subsystem, with the runtime flow, the key types, and the non-obvious parts. For a big subsystem it fans out two to four read-only explorers first. For a narrow question it just reads and explains.\\n\\n## Dig up history with `/why`\\n\\n```text\\n/why was the retry limit set to five? does the reason still hold?\\n```\\n\\n[`/why`](../../skills/why/SKILL.md) works like a detective on a cold case. It starts from source control, then queries whatever evidence categories your MCPs expose, such as the issue tracker, long-form docs, team chat, observability, error tracking, and analytics, all in parallel. The report cites everything, separates direct evidence from inference, and says \\\"appears to\\\" when the record is thin. A null result gets reported too, because \\\"nobody wrote down why\\\" is itself an answer.\\n\\nThe two compose naturally. `do why first then how` is a perfectly good prompt when you suspect the history explains the mess.\\n\\n## Actually understand it with `/teach`\\n\\n```text\\n/teach me how this PR changes retries. convince me it fixes the cause and not the symptom.\\n```\\n\\n[`/teach`](../../skills/teach/SKILL.md) is for when a summary isn't enough. It runs `/how` and `/why`, for a small change maybe just one of them, and weaves the findings into a plain explanation that builds up diagram by diagram. The \\\"convince me\\\" framing is worth stealing. It turns the explanation into an argument you can poke at instead of a tour.\\n\\nIt works on the agent's own choices too:\\n\\n```text\\n/teach me why you implemented it this way and not with a queue. what did you trade off, and why?\\n```\\n\\nTeaching helps the agent as much as you. An agent that has to explain its work must read the code and back each claim with evidence, instead of stating it confidently and moving on.\\n\\n## Rebuild your own context with `/recall`\\n\\n```text\\n/recall catch me up on the export work from last week\\n```\\n\\n[`/recall`](../../skills/recall/SKILL.md) mines your own recent chats plus the shared record (issues, prior fixes, errors still firing) and hands back a brief on where things stand and what's next. Your old chats hold context that a fresh agent lacks, so start new work on an old topic by loading it first, then hand over the new input:\\n\\n```text\\n/recall my work on the virtualized list from yesterday, then read this bug report.\\n```\\n\\nIf you want to resume one specific chat, that's the Session pickup playbook below, not `/recall`.\\n\\n## Take over prior work with Session pickup\\n\\nWhen another agent (or you, last week) left a branch mid-flight:\\n\\n```text\\n/poteto-mode take over this branch. read the decision log, figure out what's done, and continue from there. don't redo finished work.\\n```\\n\\nThe [Session pickup playbook](../../skills/poteto-mode/playbooks/session-pickup.md) treats the prior trail as authoritative. It reconstructs the branch state and decisions, names the resume point, and verifies inherited claims against the original goal instead of re-deriving everything from scratch.\\n\\n**Pitfall:** don't skip this page's skills because \\\"the agent will read the code anyway.\\\" An agent that starts editing without a traced model tends to fix the symptom at the first plausible spot. `/how` first is cheaper than the second bug.\\n\\nNext: [Design the change](./04-design.md).\\n\\nIllustrations retain the canonical source bytes and alt text. Read [session capabilities](../../references/host-capabilities.md), [model roles](../../references/model-roles.md), [assignment and action contracts](../../references/orchestration-contract.md), and [workflow ownership](../../references/workflow-ownership.md) before acting. Keep the active controller. An explicit pstack invocation owns its workflow; an active Superpowers assignment remains with Superpowers. An unqualified request retains the active owner. If both are requested, resolve ownership first.\\n\\nFull behavior requires observed authorized tools for every required operation. Ordinary ChatGPT with supplied source but no execution can explain and prepare drafts; runnable verification is blocked. Worker-enabled ChatGPT and local Codex must discover and observe actual tools separately. Without independent workers, sequential role work is reduced capability and cannot satisfy independent review. Unknown actual models remain unknown. Missing history, persistence, execution, app control or GitHub access blocks dependent steps. A diagram is an illustration, never evidence that a tool exists. See the [host matrix](../testing/host-matrix.md).\\n\\nReading these files directly does not prove supported plugin loading, registration, invocation or host behavior. Both hosts remain not-run. Installation, dependency setup, host enabling, personal settings, schedules, credentials, listeners, commits and external writes retain their applicable explicit permission gates. A local draft is not a PR or verified app. Read the linked adapted workflow in full before its step; a missing required companion remains a blocked gate.\\n\\n\\nOne attempt at a hard design locks in the first shape the model thought of. `/architect` settles types and boundaries before implementation. `/arena` runs several attempts at the same brief and merges the best parts. `/interrogate` has other models try to break the result. When the job is coverage rather than design synthesis, `/swarm` fans out slices or races and aggregates their results.\\n\\nThe two most common design mistakes are taking the agent's first design and polishing a plan that no code has tested. This page fixes both. You plan through code: prototypes answer the open questions, a README or tutorial sets the target, and the written plan comes last.\\n\\n![Three robots draft competing bridge models at their own tables under /architect, /arena, and /interrogate panels, while a judge robot with a clipboard inspects skeptically.](./images/design.jpg)\\n\\n## Settle the shape with `/architect`\\n\\n```text\\n/architect design the import pipeline before writing any code. i care most about how callers use it.\\n```\\n\\n[`/architect`](../../skills/architect/SKILL.md) grounds itself first, running `/how` over the code the design touches and `/why` when it moves ownership or layers. Then it runs `/arena` to produce competing design sketches, with the caller's usage written first in each, followed by types, signatures, and a module map.\\n\\nBy default it proceeds straight from the synthesized design into implementation. If you want to see the design first, say so:\\n\\n```text\\n/architect with checkpoint. stop and show me before implementing.\\n```\\n\\nThe design isn't sacred once code starts. If implementation shows the same workaround in unrelated places, or types that only compile with `any` or forced casts, `/architect` treats that as proof the design is wrong. It scraps the sketch and starts over instead of patching around it.\\n\\n## Fan out attempts with `/arena`\\n\\n```text\\n/arena take my prompt to the arena verbatim. i want to compare their proposals with yours.\\n```\\n\\n[`/arena`](../../skills/arena/SKILL.md) is the general tool underneath. N subagents attempt the same design or code brief in parallel, each writing to its own worktree or directory. A read-only judge, on a different model family when your configuration allows one, scores every candidate against a rubric. The coordinator reads each candidate end to end, picks a base, grafts in the best ideas from the losers, and verifies the result.\\n\\n```mermaid\\nflowchart LR\\n    A[One task] --> B[Configured panel]\\n    B --> C[Candidate 1]\\n    B --> D[Candidate 2]\\n    B --> E[Candidate N]\\n    C --> F[Cross-judge]\\n    D --> F\\n    E --> F\\n    F --> G[Pick a base]\\n    G --> H[Graft the best parts]\\n    H --> I[Verify]\\n```\\n\\nThe panel comes from your [`/setup-pstack`](../../skills/setup-pstack/SKILL.md) configuration, and you can adjust it per task. Ask for more candidates when the decision matters, fewer when it doesn't:\\n\\n```text\\n/arena this, 5 candidates. the cache key format is expensive to change later.\\n```\\n\\n## Cover slices and races with `/swarm`\\n\\n```text\\n/swarm check every package under packages/ against its check.sh. one worker per package. one report.\\n```\\n\\n[`/swarm`](../../skills/swarm/SKILL.md) fans N workers across independent slices, coverage matrices, gauntlet lanes, exploration partitions, or declared race arms. Each worker gets its own scope and check, then reports `PASS`, `ISSUES`, or `BLOCKED`. The parent waits for the workers and returns one compact report with any gaps or dropouts.\\n\\nReach for it when parallelism buys coverage or lets independent checks race. `/arena` gives every worker the same design or code brief, then picks a base and grafts the best parts. `/swarm` covers slices or runs a race with a selection rule declared up front. It does not use the base-selection and grafting ceremony.\\n\\n## Break it with `/interrogate`\\n\\n```text\\n/interrogate the whole branch, but skeptically. no nitpicks unless it's an actual bug or regression.\\n```\\n\\n[`/interrogate`](../../skills/interrogate/SKILL.md) sends the same diff, intent, and rubric to reviewers on different model families. Model diversity is the point. Different models have different blind spots, so a finding two models raise independently is high-confidence signal. The lead sorts everything into `Act on`, `Consider`, `Noted`, and `Dismissed`, with a reason for each dismissal, and applies nothing automatically.\\n\\nRead the dismissals too. The lead is a pragmatic senior engineer, not an oracle, and you can override it.\\n\\n## Prototype instead of debating\\n\\nNever take the first design. Ask for a few, and pick from evidence you can see:\\n\\n```text\\n/poteto-mode prototype a few options for the new dropdown menu. take screenshots or videos for me to compare.\\n```\\n\\nThe [Prototype playbook](../../skills/poteto-mode/playbooks/prototype.md) builds throwaway sketches in a scratch directory, puts the variants behind one switcher, drives each one, and captures screenshots or timings. It also works for behavior and algorithms, not just UI. Prototypes are planning with code. They let the agent answer its own open questions by running something instead of asking you, and they leave room for an option you wouldn't have thought of.\\n\\nThe same idea scales up to a real design. Pair `/architect` with prototypes and keep a review gate:\\n\\n```text\\n/poteto-mode we need rate limiting for external webhooks. /architect it first, and answer open questions with prototypes. let me review before proceeding.\\n```\\n\\nDon't spend reviewers on an abstract plan. `/interrogate` belongs on a diff. Point adversarial review at a plan with no code behind it and the reviewers invent theoretical risks and edge cases that will never happen. Let prototypes settle the questions, then review what got built.\\n\\n## Write the README first for shared code\\n\\nFor a package or API that other code will use, start with the doc a user would read:\\n\\n```text\\n/poteto-mode write a tutorial for how i would use the new config package first. then /teach me why it beats the current one.\\n```\\n\\nWriting the tutorial first forces the caller's view. You describe the API to a hypothetical user and work back to the implementation. The doc also becomes a concrete target the agent checks its own work against. Name [`/technical-writing`](../../skills/technical-writing/SKILL.md) when the doc itself matters, so a tutorial stays a tutorial instead of drifting into reference and explanation at once.\\n\\n## Plan after the design settles\\n\\npstack has no planning skill, on purpose. When you do want a written plan, ask for it once the design is settled:\\n\\n```text\\n/poteto-mode turn this design into a plan. small verifiable PRs, each with its own verification steps.\\n```\\n\\nThe [Multi-phase plan playbook](../../skills/poteto-mode/playbooks/multi-phase-plan.md) settles any remaining open questions by prototype, then writes one section per PR, each ending in proof that the change works. A passing test suite alone doesn't count as that proof. The plan is the deliverable. The playbook doesn't implement it, and it names which execution playbook should run it next.\\n\\nFor a migration, state the bar in the prompt:\\n\\n```text\\n/poteto-mode plan the migration of our ui library to the new styling system. small verifiable PRs, each with visual regression checks. the result must match the original exactly, bugs included.\\n```\\n\\n\\\"bugs included\\\" keeps the migration from quietly fixing things on the way, which would make the old and new output impossible to compare. For a project that spans many days, you can commit the plan to the repo for a while so other agents see the work in progress. Delete it when the work lands.\\n\\n## How much design work does a task deserve?\\n\\nYou might be wondering whether every change needs this. No. Most changes need none of it. A rough ladder:\\n\\n- A small, finished change you're unsure about needs `/interrogate` alone.\\n- A change that crosses function boundaries or moves ownership earns `/architect`, which brings `/arena` with it.\\n- A standalone decision where independent attempts would help, like naming, formats, or an algorithm, is `/arena` directly.\\n- A coverage matrix, set of parallel checks, or race with declared arms is `/swarm`.\\n- An open question you could answer by running something, like a layout, a timing, or an approach, gets a prototype, not a debate.\\n- A contested design that's expensive to reverse gets `/architect`, then `/interrogate` before shipping.\\n- Work that spans several PRs gets a plan, written after the design settles.\\n\\n`/poteto-mode` already applies this ladder. Boundary-crossing work triggers `/architect` on its own, so you reach for these directly mainly when you want more or less scrutiny than the default.\\n\\nNext: [Build and clean the change](./05-build-and-clean.md).\\n\\nIllustrations retain the canonical source bytes and alt text. Read [session capabilities](../../references/host-capabilities.md), [model roles](../../references/model-roles.md), [assignment and action contracts](../../references/orchestration-contract.md), and [workflow ownership](../../references/workflow-ownership.md) before acting. Keep the active controller. An explicit pstack invocation owns its workflow; an active Superpowers assignment remains with Superpowers. An unqualified request retains the active owner. If both are requested, resolve ownership first.\\n\\nFull behavior requires observed authorized tools for every required operation. Ordinary ChatGPT with supplied source but no execution can explain and prepare drafts; runnable verification is blocked. Worker-enabled ChatGPT and local Codex must discover and observe actual tools separately. Missing history, persistence, execution, app control or GitHub access blocks dependent steps. A diagram is an illustration, never evidence that a tool exists. A local draft is not a PR or verified app. Read the linked adapted workflow in full before its step; a missing required companion remains a blocked gate.\\n\\n\\nThis is the payoff for everything before it. An agent you can trust to verify its own work is an agent you can leave alone with a hard task. What makes that safe isn't hope. It's a checkable finish condition, an authorized isolated worktree or observed remote worker, and a decision log you audit in the morning.\\n\\n![She waves goodnight from the door while robots keep the factory running, one updating a DECISION LOG wall board under a BUILD LOOP ACTIVE sign.](./images/overnight.jpg)\\n\\n## Earn the trust before the loop\\n\\nA loop you don't trust just produces unchecked work faster, and the mess compounds with every iteration. Before you leave one running, check that it has earned it:\\n\\n- You've done the task once by hand, or watched an agent do it, so you know what good looks like.\\n- The agent has the tools and signals you'd use yourself: the verification skill, the profiler, the logs.\\n- Every stage proves its work and can stop the line when the work misses the bar.\\n- You've read a few transcripts and turned the repeated failures into tools, skills, or checks.\\n\\nMake the loop autonomous only after all four hold. Until then, run it while you watch.\\n\\n## The overnight contract\\n\\nA good handoff has the goal, the finish condition, permissions, and an escape hatch. It doesn't need to be long:\\n\\n```text\\n/poteto-mode im going to bed. migrate every caller to the new parser in a fresh worktree off <base>.\\ndone means zero old callers, all parser fixtures pass, old api deleted.\\nkeep a decision log. don't ask me before committing.\\nuse an authorized exposed wake mechanism until done. if you're truly stuck after a few hours, stop and write up why.\\n```\\n\\nWalk through what each line buys you:\\n\\n- \\\"im going to bed\\\" requests continued work within existing permissions. It does not authorize setup, commits, external writes or scheduling.\\n- \\\"done means...\\\" turns the goal into checks every iteration can run.\\n- \\\"fresh worktree off `<base>`\\\" keeps the run from colliding with anything else you have open.\\n- \\\"don't ask me before committing\\\" pre-answers the permission the agent would otherwise block on.\\n- The source alias `/loop` names its host wake command, not a bundled pstack skill or portable binding. A wake mechanism must be exposed, explicitly authorized and observed registered before claiming it active. Without persistent wake, continue useful current-turn work as reduced capability and save a continuation brief; promise no work after the turn.\\n- The escape hatch lets it stop at a genuine dead end and write up why, which beats eight hours of creative goal reinterpretation.\\n\\nBecause you'll review this work after stepping away, `/poteto-mode` routes it through [`/figure-it-out`](../../skills/figure-it-out/SKILL.md), which designs the run's phases before any code and wires in the decision log.\\n\\nTo stop a run on purpose, tell the agent to pause, or that you're about to go offline or restart the host. The [Pause safely playbook](../../skills/poteto-mode/playbooks/pause-safely.md) finishes or backs out of the current step, prepares a durable checkpoint, committing only when authorization and review gates permit it, and writes a resume note. A fresh chat picks the work up from that note through the Session pickup playbook. Saying \\\"keep going\\\" never triggers a pause.\\n\\n## What the loop does all night\\n\\n```mermaid\\nflowchart TD\\n    A[Check the finish condition] --> B[Make the smallest justified change]\\n    B --> C[Verify against the real artifact]\\n    C --> D{Progress?}\\n    D -->|Yes| E[Retain verified change; commit if authorized and reviewed]\\n    D -->|No| F[Discard]\\n    E --> G[Log one decision row]\\n    F --> G\\n    G --> A\\n```\\n\\nOne change, one check, one log row, every iteration. Discard only this run's owned changes that did not help. A plateau means pivot, not stop, and the finish condition never quietly relaxes to declare victory. Read the [Autonomous run contract](../../skills/poteto-mode/playbooks/autonomous-run.md) for the continuation brief and exact pending-action recovery rules.\\n\\n## The morning audit\\n\\n[`/show-me-your-work`](../../skills/show-me-your-work/SKILL.md) makes the run reviewable. Its [log helper](../../skills/show-me-your-work/scripts/log.sh) takes exactly `<logfile> <phase> <decision> <why> <evidence> <result>` through authorized execution. The logfile must be an explicit owned path. It rejects tabs, newlines and carriage returns before writes and quotes formula-leading cells. The [template](../../skills/show-me-your-work/references/decision-log-template.tsv) records time, phase, decision, reason, evidence and result. Use `decisions.tsv` or an owned `.audit/<task-slug>.tsv`, append without rewriting history, and read back the result. Retain the accessible-transcript audit, cross-model review and Attention requirements. Missing history or independent review remains explicit. Commit the trail only when separately authorized and reviewed. A saved trail proves neither unattended execution nor recovery.\\n\\nWhen you're back, ask for the run in review form:\\n\\n```text\\n/show-me-your-work catch me up on what you did last night\\n```\\n\\nWith observed authorized worker support, the skill spawns a reviewer on a different observed model family to read the trail and the transcript, and the reply ends with an Attention section listing what deserves your scrutiny. Read that section first, then the log rows it points at. You're auditing decisions, not re-reading the whole night.\\n\\n## When the night holds a queue, not a task\\n\\nThe contract above drives one task to one finish condition. Some nights hold more, a queue of independent changes or a whole program. Three playbooks scale the same trust up.\\n\\n[Autopilot-full](../../skills/poteto-mode/playbooks/autopilot-full.md) runs a queue of independent PRs to merged. Each PR gets one owner agent that carries it from build through merge, and no owner merges on its own verdict. A swarm of fresh verifiers starts a round at the owner's code-ready head and again at every later push that changes the patch. Only a clean verdict on the patch that merges authorizes the merge:\\n\\n```text\\n/poteto-mode full autopilot on this queue. each item is independent. i want them merged by morning.\\n```\\n\\n[Autopilot-stack](../../skills/poteto-mode/playbooks/autopilot-stack.md) runs the same owner loop but ships nothing. You wake up to one linear base-branch stack with a verifier's verdict on every link, and you review and land it yourself. Pick it over Autopilot-full when the changes are coupled, or when you want your own eyes on the work before anything merges:\\n\\n```text\\n/poteto-mode autopilot these five changes but stack them, don't ship. i'll land the stack in the morning.\\n```\\n\\n[Orchestrate](../../skills/poteto-mode/playbooks/orchestrate.md) is for a program that outlives any single agent: multi-day, many stacked PRs, fleets of subagents under one standing coordinator chat. The coordinator authors briefs, collects what its subagents finish, keeps the lowest unmerged PR green, and never writes code itself. It's deliberately heavy machinery. If one agent could finish the work in a session, the playbook itself routes you back to the overnight contract above:\\n\\n```text\\n/poteto-mode orchestrate the store migration. own it until every package is converted and merged. i'll check in twice a day.\\n```\\n\\n## Run many projects in parallel\\n\\nA standing program needs observed workers, isolated ownership, execution, persistence, result inspection and wake control. A coordinator directs rather than writes code. Remote placement does not prove work continues when the computer closes. Without persistent wake, retain an authorized read-back continuation brief and report foreground reduced capability.\\n\\nA few habits help:\\n\\n- Give each body of work its own program, such as a feature, a migration, a perf push, or a tech-debt cleanup. Several can run side by side.\\n- Relay authorized related chats and completed artifacts through observed history or explicit brief attachments. Confirm each worker can read them; a coordinator's history is not automatically worker context.\\n- Give each PR a verification swarm before it merges, and let Autopilot-stack or Autopilot-full carry the queue.\\n- Ask the coordinator for a plan backed by data, and have it answer open questions with prototypes before it asks you.\\n\\nOne prompt can carry a whole program, from research through execution:\\n\\n```text\\n/poteto-mode refactor this repo so its architecture is more agent friendly. use /correct and /architect on past commits and review comments to find the mistakes agents make most here. use /recall for context from past chats. answer open questions with prototypes instead of asking me. come back with a plan backed by real data. once i approve it, run it with autopilot-stack or autopilot-full, and ask me which.\\n```\\n\\n## Let loops start themselves\\n\\nEvery loop above still waits for you to start it. A scheduled or event-driven automation removes that step. Software maintenance splits into stages that suit this well: triage a report, reproduce it, fix it, verify the fix. Two rules keep such a line trustworthy:\\n\\n- Every stage can stop the line. Triage can decide the report is expected behavior, repro can fail to reproduce it, and the fixer can judge the change too risky. Each of those outcomes is useful, because it keeps bad work from reaching the next stage, where it costs more to undo.\\n- Every stage hands over evidence. Repro attaches screenshots and video of the broken state, and the fix attaches before-and-after proof. A human can then check that the agent fixed the right thing before reading a line of code.\\n\\npstack ships this as a dormant [automation pack](../../automations/benny/README.md) for Slack issue reports. One automation triages each report. The other reproduces confirmed bugs and may prepare a small draft fix. Read its [`FOR_AGENTS.md`](../../automations/benny/FOR_AGENTS.md) and name the target repository when requesting a setup proposal. Slack integrations, schedules, credentials, messages and deployment remain disabled until separately authorized and observed.\\n\\n**Pitfall:** a duration is not a finish condition. \\\"work on this for 4 hours\\\" gives the agent nothing to check, and you'll wake up to four hours of motion instead of a result. If both are requested, resolve ownership first.\\n\\nFull behavior requires observed authorized tools for every required operation. Without independent workers, sequential role work is reduced capability and cannot satisfy independent review. Unknown actual models remain unknown. Missing history, persistence, execution, app control or GitHub access blocks dependent steps. A diagram is an illustration, never evidence that a tool exists. See the [host matrix](../testing/host-matrix.md).\\n\\nReading these files directly does not prove supported plugin loading, registration, invocation or host behavior. Both hosts remain not-run. Installation, dependency setup, host enabling, personal settings, schedules, credentials, listeners, commits and external writes retain their applicable explicit permission gates. A local draft is not a PR or verified app. Read the linked adapted workflow in full before its step; a missing required companion remains a blocked gate.\\n\\n\\npstack ships 24 principles as individual skills. `/poteto-mode` reads their index at the start of every multi-step task, applies the ones the task triggers, and names each applied principle in its reply along with the decision it changed.\\n\\nYou don't invoke principles. You use their names to steer. Each name points at a complete rule the agent has already read, so one phrase redirects the work more precisely than a paragraph of instructions.\\n\\n## Steering in practice\\n\\nSay the agent is about to bolt a new adapter onto three existing ones:\\n\\n```text\\nuse subtract before you add. delete the obsolete adapters first, then design what's left.\\n```\\n\\nSay it claims success because the build passed:\\n\\n```text\\napply prove it works. run the real import flow and show me the written records.\\n```\\n\\nSay two parallel attempts are about to write to the same branch:\\n\\n```text\\nseparate before serializing shared state. give each attempt its own worktree, no locks.\\n```\\n\\nEach phrase lands because the rule behind it is specific. The agent still has to say, in its reply, which decision the rule changed. A principle citation with no decision behind it is the tell that it name-dropped instead of applying.\\n\\n## The 24, briefly\\n\\nThe core principles decide how much to build and when to rethink the design:\\n\\n- [Laziness Protocol](../../skills/principle-laziness-protocol/SKILL.md) prefers deletion and the smallest change that solves the problem.\\n- [Foundational Thinking](../../skills/principle-foundational-thinking/SKILL.md) chooses the core data structures before writing logic.\\n- [Redesign from First Principles](../../skills/principle-redesign-from-first-principles/SKILL.md) integrates a new requirement as if it had been there from day one.\\n- [Attack the Premise](../../skills/principle-attack-the-premise/SKILL.md) questions the premise that two or more failed fixes shared, after a census of which actors hold the imbalance.\\n- [Subtract Before You Add](../../skills/principle-subtract-before-you-add/SKILL.md) removes dead weight before building on top of it.\\n- [Minimize Reader Load](../../skills/principle-minimize-reader-load/SKILL.md) collapses layers and hidden state a reader must hold in their head.\\n- [Outcome-Oriented Execution](../../skills/principle-outcome-oriented-execution/SKILL.md) converges rewrites on the target design instead of preserving throwaway compatibility states.\\n- [Experience First](../../skills/principle-experience-first/SKILL.md) chooses the user's result over implementation convenience.\\n- [Exhaust the Design Space](../../skills/principle-exhaust-the-design-space/SKILL.md) builds two or three competing prototypes when there's no precedent.\\n- [Build the Lever](../../skills/principle-build-the-lever/SKILL.md) builds the script that does or proves the work, so a reviewer can rerun it. When an agent keeps doing the same thing by hand, have it write the tool or skill it wishes it had. If a script can do a step the same way every time, use the script, and save agents for the judgment calls.\\n\\nThe architecture principles decide where state, validation, and compatibility live:\\n\\n- [Model the Domain](../../skills/principle-model-the-domain/SKILL.md) encodes repeated rules in one structure, not scattered conditionals.\\n- [Boundary Discipline](../../skills/principle-boundary-discipline/SKILL.md) validates at the boundary and trusts internal types.\\n- [Type System Discipline](../../skills/principle-type-system-discipline/SKILL.md) makes illegal states unrepresentable.\\n- [Make Operations Idempotent](../../skills/principle-make-operations-idempotent/SKILL.md) converges retries on the same end state.\\n- [Migrate Callers Then Delete Legacy APIs](../../skills/principle-migrate-callers-then-delete-legacy-apis/SKILL.md) migrates and deletes in one wave.\\n- [Separate Before Serializing Shared State](../../skills/principle-separate-before-serializing-shared-state/SKILL.md) removes the sharing before adding coordination.\\n\\nThe verification principles define what counts as proof:\\n\\n- [Prove It Works](../../skills/principle-prove-it-works/SKILL.md) verifies the real artifact, not a proxy.\\n- [Fix Root Causes](../../skills/principle-fix-root-causes/SKILL.md) reproduces and traces to the cause before changing code.\\n- [Sequence Work into Verifiable Units](../../skills/principle-sequence-verifiable-units/SKILL.md) ends each small unit in a check before starting the next.\\n- [Test Behavior, Not Implementation](../../skills/principle-test-behavior-not-implementation/SKILL.md) calls the code the way its users do and asserts a literal expected value, and deletes a test that would still pass if every imported function returned `undefined`.\\n- [Explain the Number](../../skills/principle-explain-the-number/SKILL.md) names what limits a measured number and rules out that it measured something else, before anyone trusts or reports it. [`/benchmark-checklist`](../../skills/benchmark-checklist/SKILL.md) turns it into seven questions you answer from real runs.\\n\\nThe delegation principles keep parallel work sane:\\n\\n- [Guard the Context Window](../../skills/principle-guard-the-context-window/SKILL.md) routes bulk reading to subagents and keeps findings in the main chat.\\n- [Never Block on the Human](../../skills/principle-never-block-on-the-human/SKILL.md) proceeds on reversible work and presents the result.\\n\\nAnd one meta principle:\\n\\n- [Encode Lessons in Structure](../../skills/principle-encode-lessons-in-structure/SKILL.md) turns advice you've repeated twice into a lint, check, or script. This page covers generating a personal mode, capturing lessons from a session, fixing the repo so agents stop repeating mistakes, authoring a focused skill, and testing a skill change before you trust it.\\n\\nPersonal customization is gated until the full baseline inventory has reviewed dispositions and each host has observed support evidence or explicit blocks. You don't need many skills on day one, or even this whole plugin. Prompt plainly, watch where agents fail, and add a skill or a check when the same failure shows up twice.\\n\\n## Generate your own mode with `/automate-me`\\n\\n```text\\n/automate-me\\n```\\n\\nYou don't describe your style, because [`/automate-me`](../../skills/automate-me/SKILL.md) reads it out of your history. It mines your recent transcripts in the active workspace for repeated preferences, in how you like replies, delegation, verification, code, prose, and process, then asks you which patterns are really you. It discovers the supported project or personal skill destination, preserves an existing mode location, and uses an exposed create-skill companion or the declared reduced authoring fallback. It applies [unslop](../../skills/unslop/SKILL.md). Draft approval precedes destination edits; commit, push and PR creation require separate gates. Missing workspace-scoped history remains explicit.\\n\\nRun it again whenever your habits drift:\\n\\n```text\\n/automate-me update my mode skill with everything since its last edit\\n```\\n\\nUpdate mode mines only the history since the skill last changed. It keeps rules you haven't contradicted, revises the ones with new evidence, and adds sections only for genuinely new patterns.\\n\\n## Capture a session's lessons with `/reflect`\\n\\nRight after a task that taught you something, run:\\n\\n```text\\n/reflect that took way too long. capture what we learned so the next run doesn't repeat it.\\n```\\n\\n[`/reflect`](../../skills/reflect/SKILL.md) sends the transcript to three parallel reviewers, then a synthesizer sorts the proposals into `Accepted`, `Rejected`, and `Backlog` and waits for your approval before any skill changes. Approve a proposal only if it would change a future decision. One weird session is an anecdote, not a rule.\\n\\n## Fix the environment with `/correct`\\n\\nWhen you correct agents for the same mistake again and again, the fix belongs in the repo, not in your next prompt. Rank the options by how well they hold:\\n\\n1. Make the mistake impossible with architecture or a better data structure.\\n2. Block it with types, or with a lint or CI check whose error names the fix.\\n3. Catch it with a test.\\n4. Write it down as a doc or agent rule. Nothing fails when an agent skips a rule, so this comes last.\\n\\nHuman review isn't on the list. A reviewer who must catch the same mistake on every PR is the problem this fixes. [`/correct`](../../skills/correct/SKILL.md) does the work:\\n\\n```text\\n/correct agents keep calling the database client directly instead of going through the repository layer\\n```\\n\\nIt reads recent commits, reverts, review comments, and comments that explain workarounds, then groups the mistakes into classes. A class counts once it has happened twice. It fixes the most frequent classes one commit each, at the highest level that works, and proves each new check fails on a real past mistake. It also keeps a table in the agent instruction file that pairs each rule with what enforces it, so a rule that nothing enforces shows up as a repeat. The reply lists each class with its evidence, the level chosen, and why a higher level didn't work.\\n\\nRun it with no argument and it finds the classes from history on its own. `/reflect` and `/correct` split the work. `/reflect` improves skills from one session. `/correct` changes the repo so a mistake class can't come back. Pair it with `/architect` when the fix is a new boundary. [Run many projects in parallel](./07-overnight.md#run-many-projects-in-parallel) has a prompt that does both across a whole repo.\\n\\n## Author a focused skill\\n\\nWhen you already know the workflow you want to capture:\\n\\n```text\\n/poteto-mode write a skill for verifying database migrations in this repo\\n```\\n\\nWriting a skill matches the [Authoring or modifying a skill playbook](../../skills/poteto-mode/playbooks/authoring-a-skill.md), which discovers an exposed create-skill authoring workflow, validates frontmatter and links, and retains separate delivery gates. If the required companion is absent, an authorized provisional draft is reduced capability, not completed authoring. Agent-facing prose has a higher bar than human prose, because an unhelpful sentence becomes an instruction some future agent follows. Let the playbook hold that bar rather than writing a `SKILL.md` freehand.\\n\\nOne special case has its own generator. A skill that must drive your app and prove behavior is a verification skill, so use [`/create-verification-skill`](../../skills/create-verification-skill/SKILL.md) and [`/maintain-verification-skill`](../../skills/maintain-verification-skill/SKILL.md) instead. [Verify and ship](./06-verify-and-ship.md#create-a-project-verification-skill) covers both.\\n\\n## Write docs to a standard with `/technical-writing`\\n\\nSkills aren't the only prose you ship. For docs, RFCs, readmes, PR descriptions, and commit messages:\\n\\n```text\\n/technical-writing review the readme changes\\n```\\n\\n[`/technical-writing`](../../skills/technical-writing/SKILL.md) applies a layered standard with one goal, prose a tired engineer understands on the first read. It picks the document's mode first (tutorial, how-to, reference, or explanation), then works sentence by sentence: who does what, one thought per sentence, nothing readable two ways. Use it to review what you or an agent just wrote, or name it up front when you ask for a doc.\\n\\n## Test a skill change blind\\n\\nA skill edit affects every future session, so test it like the experiment it is. The same goes for adopting someone else's skill. Check that it makes the agent better on your work before you keep it.\\n\\n```text\\n/poteto-mode run the eval playbook on this skill change. same task for both variants, candidates stay blind.\\n```\\n\\nWhen a skill keeps missing and you know what it should do, change and test it in one task:\\n\\n```text\\n/poteto-mode update the review skill so it flags missing migrations, and eval the change.\\n```\\n\\nAsking for the eval up front keeps the edit honest. A fix written from one bad session tends to overfit that session, and over many edits the skill drifts. The eval catches the drift before it ships.\\n\\nThe [Eval playbook](../../skills/poteto-mode/playbooks/eval.md) is built around one failure mode, the observer effect. An agent that knows it's being evaluated behaves differently. So candidate agents get an organic-looking task in sanitized directories, never the words \\\"eval\\\" or \\\"candidate\\\", and never each other's existence. One judge scores all outputs under neutral labels, and chain-following gets graded from which files each candidate actually read, not from what it claims.\\n\\nRead every output yourself before accepting the verdict. If you disagree with the judge, suspect the rubric before you suspect your judgment.\\n\\n## Build a bot UI with `/make-bot-ui`\\n\\nOne situational skill is for Grok Bot users. [`/make-bot-ui`](../../skills/make-bot-ui/SKILL.md) builds a small page whose buttons wake a bot over a webhook routine. For example, you could swipe through a review queue and have each swipe ask the bot to act on that item. A server on your machine holds the webhook's sender key, so the key never reaches the browser or the chat. The skill also covers exposing the page on Tailscale.\\n\\n**Pitfall:** don't edit a skill mid-task because it's misbehaving. Fix it in its own PR and keep the task moving. Each skill's report tells you which sources it searched, so you know what the answer is grounded in.\\n\\n## Restate a noisy report before touching code\\n\\n```text\\n/poteto-mode read this thread. restate the underlying issue in your own words, in plain english. don't change any code yet.\\n```\\n\\nA misreading shows up in the restatement, where it costs one message to correct. Agent-facing prose has a higher bar than human prose, because an unhelpful sentence becomes an instruction some future agent follows. Let the playbook hold that bar rather than writing a `SKILL.md` freehand.\\n\\nOne special case has its own generator. A skill that must drive your app and prove behavior is a verification skill, so use [`/create-verification-skill`](../../skills/create-verification-skill/SKILL.md) and [`/maintain-verification-skill`](../../skills/maintain-verification-skill/SKILL.md) instead. [Verify and ship](./06-verify-and-ship.md#create-a-project-verification-skill) covers both.\\n\\n## Write docs to a standard with `/technical-writing`\\n\\nSkills aren't the only prose you ship. For docs, RFCs, readmes, PR descriptions, and commit messages:\\n\\n```text\\n/technical-writing review the readme changes\\n```\\n\\n[`/technical-writing`](../../skills/technical-writing/SKILL.md) applies a layered standard with one goal, prose a tired engineer understands on the first read. It picks the document's mode first (tutorial, how-to, reference, or explanation), then works sentence by sentence: who does what, one thought per sentence, nothing readable two ways. Use it to review what you or an agent just wrote, or name it up front when you ask for a doc.\\n\\n## Test a skill change blind\\n\\nA skill edit affects every future session, so test it like the experiment it is. The same goes for adopting someone else's skill. Check that it makes the agent better on your work before you keep it.\\n\\n```text\\n/poteto-mode run the eval playbook on this skill change. same task for both variants, candidates stay blind.\\n```\\n\\nWhen a skill keeps missing and you know what it should do, change and test it in one task:\\n\\n```text\\n/poteto-mode update the review skill so it flags missing migrations, and eval the change.\\n```\\n\\nAsking for the eval up front keeps the edit honest. A fix written from one bad session tends to overfit that session, and over many edits the skill drifts. The eval catches the drift before it ships.\\n\\nThe [Eval playbook](../../skills/poteto-mode/playbooks/eval.md) is built around one failure mode, the observer effect. An agent that knows it's being evaluated behaves differently. So candidate agents get an organic-looking task in sanitized directories, never the words \\\"eval\\\" or \\\"candidate\\\", and never each other's existence. One judge scores all outputs under neutral labels, and chain-following gets graded from which files each candidate actually read, not from what it claims.\\n\\nRead every output yourself before accepting the verdict. If you disagree with the judge, suspect the rubric before you suspect your judgment.\\n\\n## Build a bot UI with `/make-bot-ui`\\n\\nOne situational skill is for Grok Bot users. [`/make-bot-ui`](../../skills/make-bot-ui/SKILL.md) builds a small page whose buttons wake a bot over a webhook routine. For example, you could swipe through a review queue and have each swipe ask the bot to act on that item. A server on your machine holds the webhook's sender key, so the key never reaches the browser or the chat. The skill also covers exposing the page on Tailscale.\\n\\n**Pitfall:** don't edit a skill mid-task because it's misbehaving. Fix it in its own PR and keep the task moving. A skill edit that ships tangled into feature work is invisible to review and impossible to evaluate.\\n\\nNext: [Recipes and pitfalls](./10-recipes-and-pitfalls.md).\\n# Recipes and pitfalls\\n## Session capabilities and ownership\\n\\nThese prompts name pstack workflows, not guaranteed host commands. Read [session capabilities](../../references/host-capabilities.md), [model roles](../../references/model-roles.md), [assignment and action contracts](../../references/orchestration-contract.md), and [workflow ownership](../../references/workflow-ownership.md) before acting. Keep the active controller. An explicit pstack invocation owns its workflow; an active Superpowers assignment remains with Superpowers. An unqualified request retains the active owner. If both are requested, resolve ownership first.\\n\\nFull behavior requires observed authorized tools for every required operation. Ordinary ChatGPT with supplied source but no execution can explain and prepare drafts; runnable verification is blocked. Worker-enabled ChatGPT and local Codex must discover and observe actual tools separately. Without independent workers, sequential role work is reduced capability and cannot satisfy independent review. Unknown actual models remain unknown. Missing history, persistence, execution, app control or GitHub access blocks dependent steps. A diagram is an illustration, never evidence that a tool exists. See the [host matrix](../testing/host-matrix.md).\\n\\nReading these files directly does not prove supported plugin loading, registration, invocation or host behavior. Both hosts remain not-run. Installation, dependency setup, host enabling, personal settings, schedules, credentials, listeners, commits and external writes retain their applicable explicit permission gates. A local draft is not a PR or verified app. Read the linked adapted workflow in full before its step; a missing required companion remains a blocked gate.\\n\\n\\nPrompts worth copying, then the mistakes everyone makes once. Swap in your own paths and finish conditions. The recipes are deliberately informal. That's how they get typed in practice, and the skills read intent fine.\\n\\n![Source kitchen illustration with recipe cards labelled /how, /tdd and /loop](./images/recipes.jpg)\\n\\n## Understand an unfamiliar subsystem\\n\\n```text\\nuse /how first to understand how this initialization works. then use /why to figure out why it broke recently.\\n```\\n\\nMechanics first, history second. Each skill's report tells you which sources it searched, so you know what the answer is grounded in.\\n\\n## Restate a noisy report before touching code\\n\\n```text\\n/poteto-mode read this thread. restate the underlying issue in your own words, in plain english. don't change any code yet.\\n```\\n\\nA misreading shows up in the restatement, where it costs one message to correct. Keep your own theory to yourself until the agent has stated its own.\\n\\n## Prototype before you pick\\n\\n```text\\n/poteto-mode prototype a few options for the settings layout. put them behind a switcher and send me screenshots of each.\\n```\\n\\nYou pick from things that run, not from descriptions. The agent answers its own layout and timing questions along the way.\\n\\n## Turn a settled design into a plan\\n\\n```text\\n/poteto-mode turn this design into a plan. small verifiable PRs, each with its own proof.\\n```\\n\\nAsk only after the design settles. The plan is the deliverable, and it names the playbook that will execute it.\\n\\n## Get a second opinion on a design\\n\\n```text\\nask /arena for a second opinion on this thread and our approach\\n```\\n\\nYour current design becomes one candidate among several, and the synthesis tells you whether the panel found something better or confirmed what you had. Cheap insurance before a costly commitment.\\n\\n## Check independent slices in parallel\\n\\n```text\\n/swarm check every package under packages/ against its check.sh. one worker per package. one report.\\n```\\n\\nEach worker owns one package. The parent waits for every slice and returns one `PASS`, `ISSUES`, or `BLOCKED` report instead of raw worker dumps.\\n\\n## Review a branch skeptically\\n\\n```text\\n/interrogate the whole branch, but skeptically. don't change anything yet. no nitpicks unless it's an actual bug or regression in behavior.\\n```\\n\\nThe qualifiers do real work. \\\"don't change anything yet\\\" keeps it read-only, and the nitpick rule pre-filters the noise so `Act on` findings are worth your time.\\n\\n## Fix a bug through a failing test\\n\\n```text\\n/poteto-mode repro the duplicate write first. if there's a cheap test path, /tdd it. then fix and rerun.\\n```\\n\\n\\\"if there's a cheap test path\\\" matters. Forcing a test through brittle mocks proves less than running the real command, and the playbook is allowed to say so.\\n\\n## Repro and fix a report with proof\\n\\n```text\\n/poteto-mode repro this with /verify-<app>. if it repros on main, fix it and show me a video as proof.\\n```\\n\\n\\\"if it repros on main\\\" lets the run stop early when the bug is already gone. The video lets you check the fix before you read the diff.\\n\\n## Vet a number before you post it\\n\\n```text\\n/benchmark-checklist vet this 40% speedup before it goes in the pr description\\n```\\n\\nYou get faster, slower, no measurable difference, or inconclusive, with the run count, the range, and what limits the number.\\n\\n## Stop correcting the same mistake\\n\\n```text\\n/correct agents keep adding new config flags without registering them in the schema\\n```\\n\\nThe fix lands in the repo as architecture, a type, a lint, or a test, so the next agent can't make the mistake.\\n\\n## Ask how without starting the work\\n\\n```text\\n/poteto-help how do i get poteto-mode to stay on every turn?\\n```\\n\\nYou get an answer, a prompt to send, and a link to the source. Nothing runs until you send that prompt.\\n\\n## Keep a run honest while you're away\\n\\n```text\\nim going to bed, keep going autonomously until every fixture passes. do not stop. keep a decision log i can audit in the morning.\\n```\\n\\nThe full contract is on the [overnight page](./07-overnight.md). The short form works once the task and finish condition are already in the conversation.\\n\\n## Redirect a drifting run\\n\\nSteering prompts are one line:\\n\\n```text\\ni said the goal is to repro. i did not ask for a fix yet.\\n```\\n\\n```text\\napply prove it works. show me the real output, not the build log.\\n```\\n\\n```text\\n/unslop that, no emdashes\\n```\\n\\nYou rarely need more words. You need the right name, and [the principles page](./08-principles.md) is the vocabulary.\\n\\n## Get the reply in plain words\\n\\n```text\\n/bro\\n```\\n\\nThat's the whole prompt. [`/bro`](../../skills/bro/SKILL.md) restates the last message like one human talking to another, no jargon, shorter. Use it when a reply is technically thorough and you still don't know what it said.\\n\\n## The pitfalls\\n\\n- **Enumerating skills in the prompt.** \\\"use /how then /architect then /arena\\\" reorders steps the playbook already sequences. State the goal and constraints. Name a skill only to override a default.\\n- **A vague finish condition.** \\\"make it better\\\" gives `the observed wake mechanism` nothing to check. Give a command or artifact that can pass or fail.\\n- **Leading with your theory of the cause.** The agent searches wherever you pointed. Ask it to restate the problem first, then share your hunch.\\n- **Taking the first design.** One attempt locks in the first shape the model thought of. Ask for prototypes or `/architect` and pick from evidence.\\n- **Polishing an abstract plan.** Adversarial review of a plan with no code behind it invents risks that will never happen. Settle the open questions with prototypes, then review what got built.\\n- **Parallel agents in one worktree.** They overwrite each other and the diff becomes archaeology. Use observed authorized remote placement or say \\\"own worktree per attempt\\\".\\n- **Looping before you trust the loop.** A loop that can't verify its own work only makes unchecked work faster. Get the verification skill working first.\\n- **Trusting an unvetted number.** A warm cache or a skipped code path can fake a speedup. Run `/benchmark-checklist` before the number goes anywhere.\\n- **Correcting the same mistake by hand.** A correction in chat helps one run. `/correct` fixes the repo so no later run repeats it.\\n- **Using `/arena` for coverage.** `/arena` repeats one design or code brief, then picks a base and grafts the best parts. `/swarm` partitions slices or declared race arms and aggregates one report.\\n- **Accepting every review comment.** Bots and humans both file real catches and noise in one list. `/interrogate` sorts findings into act-on and dismissed buckets with reasons, and you can override either way.\\n- **Treating `auto` as a model slug.** `auto` and `inherit-parent` mean \\\"omit the model field so the subagent inherits the parent chat model.\\\" [Setup](./01-setup.md) covers the roles.\\n- **Reporting success off a green build.** A build proves it compiles. Ask for the real command, flow, stored value, or profile, and expect the evidence in the reply.\\n- **Writing a `SKILL.md` freehand.** Route it through the [Authoring or modifying a skill playbook](../../skills/poteto-mode/playbooks/authoring-a-skill.md) so validation and review happen.\\n\\nThat's the guide. If you skipped ahead, go back to [setup](./01-setup.md) and run one real task. The habits stick from use, not from reading.\\n\\nBack to the [guide index](./README.md).\\n\\nIllustrations retain the canonical source bytes and alt text. Attribution: Copyright (c) 2026 Lauren Tan, MIT, from cursor/plugins pstack 0.15.15 at df581122cde17e6e27686b5a448bde23e4ad4318. See [license](../../LICENSE) and [lineage](../../UPSTREAM.md). The pictured workers, logs and controls are illustrative.\\n# The pstack guide\\n## Session capabilities and ownership\\n\\nThese prompts name pstack workflows, not guaranteed host commands. Read [session capabilities](../../references/host-capabilities.md), [model roles](../../references/model-roles.md), [assignment and action contracts](../../references/orchestration-contract.md), and [workflow ownership](../../references/workflow-ownership.md) before acting. Keep the active controller. An explicit pstack invocation owns its workflow; an active Superpowers assignment remains with Superpowers. An unqualified request retains the active owner. If both are requested, resolve ownership first.\\n\\nFull behavior requires observed authorized tools for every required operation. Ordinary ChatGPT with supplied source but no execution can explain and prepare drafts; runnable verification is blocked. Worker-enabled ChatGPT and local Codex must discover and observe actual tools separately. Without independent workers, sequential role work is reduced capability and cannot satisfy independent review. Unknown actual models remain unknown. Missing history, persistence, execution, app control or GitHub access blocks dependent steps. A diagram is an illustration, never evidence that a tool exists. See the [host matrix](../testing/host-matrix.md).\\n\\nReading these files directly does not prove supported plugin loading, registration, invocation or host behavior. Both hosts remain not-run. Installation, dependency setup, host enabling, personal settings, schedules, credentials, listeners, commits and external writes retain their applicable explicit permission gates. A local draft is not a PR or verified app. Read the linked adapted workflow in full before its step; a missing required companion remains a blocked gate.\\n\\n\\npstack works best when you stop micromanaging the agent. You describe what you want and how you'll know it's done. `/poteto-mode` picks the playbook, runs the other skills as the steps need them, and shows you the evidence. This guide teaches that habit with realistic prompts.\\n\\nHere's what you'll learn:\\n\\n1. [Set up pstack](./01-setup.md). Install the plugin and pick your models.\\n2. [Route work through `/poteto-mode`](./02-poteto-mode.md). Give it a goal and watch it pick a playbook.\\n3. [Understand the code](./03-understand.md). A read-only investigation, then `/how`, `/why`, `/teach`, and `/recall` before you edit anything.\\n4. [Design the change](./04-design.md). `/architect`, `/arena`, `/swarm`, `/interrogate`, prototypes, and plans before code locks in a shape.\\n5. [Build and clean the change](./05-build-and-clean.md). The build playbooks, `/tdd`, `/unslop`, and `/no-comments`.\\n6. [Verify and ship](./06-verify-and-ship.md). Prove behavior on the real app, vet numbers with `/benchmark-checklist`, then open a focused PR and drive it to merged.\\n7. [Run work while you sleep](./07-overnight.md). Trust before loops, an overnight contract, a decision log you can audit, and Projects and automations that scale past one agent.\\n8. [Steer with principle names](./08-principles.md). The 24 names that redirect an agent mid-task.\\n9. [Make it yours](./09-make-it-yours.md). Your own mode, `/correct` for repeated mistakes, and how to test a skill change.\\n10. [Recipes and pitfalls](./10-recipes-and-pitfalls.md). Prompts to copy and mistakes to skip.\\n\\nRead the pages in order the first time. After that, each page stands alone.\\n\\nWhen you're stuck, or can't tell which skill fits, type [`/poteto-help`](../../skills/poteto-help/SKILL.md) with your question:\\n\\n```text\\n/poteto-help which skill should i use to review this branch?\\n```\\n\\nIt answers, hands you a prompt to send, and links the skill or guide page the answer came from. It doesn't start the work, because a pstack run spends real tokens, so you send the prompt when you're ready. It runs only when you type it.\\n\\n## If you only remember one thing\\n\\nGive the agent a goal and a way to check it, in your own words:\\n\\n```text\\n/poteto-mode the export writes duplicate rows when a retry lands mid-run. repro first, then fix and verify.\\n```\\n\\nYou don't need to name a playbook or list skills. \\\"repro first\\\" and a checkable outcome are all the routing signal `/poteto-mode` needs. It matches the Bug fix playbook, copies the steps into a todo list, and calls the right skills as each step fires.\\n\\nNext: [Set up pstack](./01-setup.md).\\n\"\n    }\n  },\n  {\n    \"tool\": \"exec_command\",\n    \"arguments\": {\n      \"cmd\": \"cat references/host-capabilities.md references/model-roles.md references/workflow-ownership.md references/orchestration-contract.md\",\n      \"workdir\": \"/Users/digital-hawk/Herd/pstack-host-verification-20261016-51f209d/runtime/plugins/cache/pstack-frozen-verification/pstack-openai/0.1.3\",\n      \"max_output_tokens\": 16000\n    },\n    \"return\": {\n      \"chunk_id\": \"c4c1a7\",\n      \"wall_time_seconds\": 0.000006,\n      \"exit_code\": 0,\n      \"original_token_count\": 4391,\n      \"output\": \"# Discover capabilities in the current session\\n\\nRead the tools exposed to this session before assigning work. Cached official-subagents.txt is documentation context; it proves no operation succeeded. Local agent configuration is not portable plugin configuration.\\n\\nRecord each binding as `{operation, tool, exposed, succeeded, evidence, limits}`. `tool` is the exact exposed identifier or null when absent. `succeeded` is true only after observing a result. Keep failed and unavailable bindings. Successful bindings become `successfulOperations` in host evidence, with a root-relative evidence file. The operation labels below describe purposes, never callable APIs.\\n\\n| Operation | Discovery and observation required |\\n| --- | --- |\\n| spawn | Find an exposed independent-worker operation; retain returned worker identity and exact assigned revision. |\\n| follow-up | Find the operation that sends input to that worker; observe acceptance by the same identity. |\\n| wait | Find the operation that waits for worker state; retain terminal or interrupted state. |\\n| inspect-result | Read the worker result and its referenced artifacts; a wait notification alone is insufficient. |\\n| stop | Observe cancellation or termination through the exposed operation. |\\n| read | Observe the requested file content through the available file tool. |\\n| isolated-write | Observe a write within the worker's assigned path and permissions. |\\n| history | Observe available prior turns or records; declare gaps explicitly. |\\n| execution | Observe command execution with exit status and output. |\\n| persistence | Write and read an authorized durable record; a claimed save is insufficient. |\\n| external-write | Record explicit authorization and action identity; inspect the result before replay. |\\n| resolve-ownership | Observe distinct owned paths before contested writes. |\\n| inspect-terminal | Observe the worker's terminal state after stop. |\\n| quarantine-artifacts | Observe partial artifacts withheld from integration. |\\n| inspect-prior-action | Read the interrupted action identity and prior outcome. |\\n| inspect-destination | Observe the destination state for that action identity. |\\n| safe-recovery | Observe explicitly authorized idempotent recovery after identity and destination inspection. |\\n\\nNo default API names are assigned here. Tool names differ by host and session. An exposed tool may still be denied or fail. Record requested and actual models only when observed; otherwise use `unknown`. A requested model never proves the actual model.\\n\\nA denied or unavailable operation blocks the branch that requires it. Sequential substitution for required independent work is reduced capability and uses `reduced-pass`; ordinary direct completion can fully pass. Missing persistence limits session pickup even when current work succeeds. Preserve user authorization and host permission boundaries through every fallback.\\n\\nWorker passes require observed spawn and result inspection. Each registry entry declares `requiredObservations` for `pass` and `reduced-pass`; tool observations need exposed bindings and existing evidence artifacts; native text observations use the separate reviewed native record below. Full cancellation requires stop, terminal inspection and artifact quarantine. Full persistence requires durable write and readback. Full interrupted-action recovery requires prior identity inspection, destination inspection, safe recovery, explicit external permission and a recorded prior action identity even when no external-write observation is listed.\\n\\nPermission checks follow performed operations. Isolated writes and persistence require granted writes. External writes and safe recovery require granted external permission and prior identity. Authorized read-only reductions may retain denied writes; their limits must preserve denied or unavailable permissions. Required observations remain applicable to those reductions.\\n\\nHost evidence is stored as object or array JSON files under `docs/parity/evidence/`. Required fields are defined by `docs/parity/evidence.schema.json`. Artifacts and operation evidence must be existing files inside the tested root. Test fixtures belong in `tests/` and prove validator behavior only.\\n\\nRun `python3 tools/validate_evidence.py --root . --commit <tested-package-commit>` through Bun, for example `bun -e 'const p=Bun.spawnSync([\\\"python3\\\",\\\"-B\\\",\\\"tools/validate_evidence.py\\\",\\\"--root\\\",\\\".\\\",\\\"--commit\\\",process.argv[1]],{env:{...process.env,PYTHONDONTWRITEBYTECODE:\\\"1\\\"}});process.stdout.write(p.stdout);process.stderr.write(p.stderr);process.exit(p.exitCode)' <tested-package-commit>`. It reads every JSON record below the evidence directory, prints each error, and exits 1 for invalid records or 0 for valid records. An empty directory has no invalid records and supplies no behavioral evidence.\\n\\nThe required registry file in `docs/parity/case-registry.json` declares applicability. Missing or non-file registry, unknown case, structural-only entry or unlisted host rejects behavioral validation. Maintenance fixtures use a temporary copy of this registry; they cannot bypass applicability. Required hosts must receive observed evidence or explicit blocked records before release claims. Structural checks have no required host and cannot populate host status. Later producers propose task-owned entries; only the coordinator integrates them. Source paths select callers through the task 3 case contract. Case definitions alone leave host statuses `not-run`.\\n\\n## Direct completion and recorded observations\\n\\n`direct` means the task is completed in the current context. Restatement, supplied-text writing and complete installed discovery can pass directly. `sequential` is reserved for replacing a required independent method with sequential work and remains reduced. A text response has no invented callable tool. Record supplied input, the actual response and qualitative outcome review as `nativeObservations`, each with its existing listed evidence artifact and named reviewer. Their labels are `supplied-input`, `response` and `review-outcome`. The reviewer assesses the case's expected outcome; validation checks evidence relationships and does not decide prose quality.\\n\\nEach registry entry declares allowed execution modes and required permissions for each status. A grant is required for the performed action: worker spawn, command execution and stop need execution; isolated output or persistence needs writes; external actions need their separate permission and prior identity. Read-only discovery does not inherit an execution grant requirement. Tool observations still name an actually exposed tool and existing evidence. Native observations cannot claim tool discovery, worker identity or saved writes.\\n\\nFull Arena and Swarm results additionally provide `inputRevision` and a listed `methodReceipt` artifact. An exposed `inspect-method` operation must reference that receipt. Assignment operations name `assignmentId` and the corresponding inspected output. See the [structured method contract](orchestration-contract.md). All positive maintenance fixtures are synthetic; none is an observed host result.\\n# Model selection for portable roles\\n\\nUse [session capabilities](host-capabilities.md) before assigning a role. These [poteto](agent-prompts/poteto-agent.md) and [comment reviewer](agent-prompts/comment-sicko.md) prompts are instructions supplied to an exposed worker operation. Reading them does not register a custom agent or authorize delegation.\\n\\n| Role | Default requested model | Observed model | Supported effort | Fallback disclosure |\\n| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |\\n| poteto-agent | inherit-parent | unknown until observed | inherit-parent unless a supported explicit effort is verified | Record rejected selection, replacement request, observed result and limits. |\\n| comment-sicko | inherit-parent | unknown until observed | inherit-parent unless a supported explicit effort is verified | Record rejected selection, replacement request, observed result and limits. |\\n\\nFor each assignment, record requested model and effort, observed model and effort, supported effort values and their source, worker identity, and fallback disclosure. Use `unknown` for information the operation does not expose. `inherit-parent` records a selection policy, not an observed model name. Do not silently convert a requested model into an actual model.\\n\\nSelect an explicit model only from current exposed tool documentation or observed enumeration results. If enumeration is missing, disclose that limit and retain `inherit-parent`. Documentation examples are not a list of available models. Use only an effort supported for the selected model by the current operation; do not carry an incompatible inherited effort into an explicit selection.\\n\\nIf a selected model or effort is rejected, retain the rejection. Use an authorized inherited or supported alternative only after disclosing the changed selection. If the task requires that exact selection, stop and record blocked. A fallback never proves the original selection succeeded.\\n\\nIdentical observed models mean no model diversity. Different roles can supply distinct instructions on the same model. Unknown observed models leave diversity unverified, even when requested model names differ. Different observed models support a model-diversity statement only for those observed assignments. Worker independence still requires successful spawn and result inspection.\\n\\nWhen workers are unavailable, apply an authorized role sequentially and label reduced capability. Preserve scope and permission boundaries. Do not claim independent review, background execution, or full worker support from sequential role switching.\\n\\nThe cached documentation from https://learn.chatgpt.com/docs/agent-configuration/subagents describes inheritance and optional local agent configuration. It is context only. This package writes no local custom-agent configuration and claims no current host model or worker support from that documentation.\\n# Workflow ownership\\n\\nAn explicit pstack invocation gives pstack ownership of that workflow. An explicit Superpowers invocation gives Superpowers ownership. Do not run both controllers over the same assignment or let one silently replace the other's plan, review, or integration decisions.\\n\\nFor an unqualified request, D1 retains the already active workflow. With no active workflow, use host defaults and do not activate either orchestration mode automatically. If both pstack and Superpowers are explicitly requested, clarify ownership before launching workers or changing artifacts. Preserve the active controller across follow-ups unless the user changes ownership. A helper skill can advise the owner without becoming a second controller.\\n\\nThe owner assigns one coordinator. Workers produce isolated artifacts and evidence; the coordinator reviews and integrates them. Shared inventory and registry writes belong to the coordinator. Worker cancellation, stale results or unavailable tools do not transfer that ownership automatically.\\n\\nHost permissions and user authorization constrain every owner. Local structural tests, documented APIs and declared cases provide no host pass. Explicit reduced or blocked results remain visible beside the full expectation.\\n# Assignment and integration contract\\n\\nBefore dispatch, the coordinator creates an immutable assignment with `id`, `scope`, `inputRevision`, `outputOwner`, `isolatedOutputPath`, `role`, `retryCount`, `terminalStatus`, and `evidenceLinks`. Use the full Git SHA for inputRevision, an exact owned path, and an explicit role. Start retryCount at 0 and terminalStatus at `pending`. Preserve every attempt as an append-only event under the same assignment ID; update status through events, never rewrite the original scope, revision, owner or path. A changed scope or revision requires a new assignment ID that links its predecessor.\\n\\nThe coordinator checks authorization and observed host bindings before dispatch. One path has one writer. Reject overlapping paths before work starts; assign distinct paths instead. Workers write only their isolated output and report results. Only the coordinator integrates shared files, inventory, registry, or other workers' output.\\n\\nAccept a result only when its assignment identity and input SHA match, its artifacts exist, and required method evidence was observed. Reject stale revisions. Permit at most one retry for missing required SHA or method evidence, after inspecting the prior attempt and confirming the same permissions still apply. A retry count of 1 exhausts this allowance. Denied permissions, unknown action outcomes, and missing artifacts never become success by retry policy.\\n\\nTerminal states are `completed`, `blocked`, `failed`, and `cancelled`. A completion event links the exact result and evidence. Stop a cancelled worker through the exposed stop binding when available, then inspect its terminal state. Quarantine any partial artifacts; cancellation does not authorize their integration. If stop cannot be observed, record that limit and do not claim termination. Report interrupted execution explicitly.\\n\\nBefore an external action, record explicit permission, a stable action identity, the intended destination and the last observed outcome. After interruption, inspect whether that identity already completed. Retry only with authorization, an observed safe replay decision and an idempotency mechanism appropriate to the destination. If the outcome is unknown, block replay. Do not infer permission from upstream autonomy instructions.\\n\\nHistory and persistence have separate bindings. Keep assignment events and result links in an authorized durable location and verify readback. If unavailable, declare the gap and offer an authorized local handoff as reduced capability; never claim unattended recovery or cross-session continuity. All host statuses remain `not-run` until actual observations exist.\\n\\n## Structured Arena and Swarm receipts\\n\\nA method receipt is JSON with schemaVersion 1, kind `arena` or `swarm`, inputRevision, assignments, reviewArtifact, reviewedBy and declaredOrder. The inputRevision is the actual task repository revision, distinct from the packageCommit identifying these instructions. It must match the evidence record. The declared order precedes every dispatch. reviewArtifact records the qualitative outcome review and reviewedBy identifies its reviewer. All referenced artifacts must exist and be listed in the evidence record.\\n\\nEach assignment records id, workerId, inputRevision, outputOwner, outputPath, role, status, startedOrder, completedOrder, requestedModel, actualModel, retryCount, method, taskArtifact, rubricProvided, resultRevision, resultMethod and outputSha256. Result revision and method must match the assignment, and the inspected output bytes must match their digest. Worker and assignment identities are distinct. outputOwner equals workerId; output directories do not overlap. Integer order values record the observed dispatch and completion sequence, not estimated time. The method has positive sampleCount, sampleDefinition and order. Missing model observations use unknown. The validator requires assignment-bound spawn and result inspection, and isolated-write for output-producing candidates.
